> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the Load Topology of PKM Workflows?
> **Answer:** An original framework (this report) that maps PKM workflow phases (Capture → Structure → Connect → Retrieve) to their dominant load type and risk, and the corresponding optimal load management strategy. Capture: risk of extraneous load from simultaneous transcription + comprehension; strategy: selective abstraction with deliberate delay. Structure: risk of intrinsic load from 과high element-interactivity notes; strategy: atomicity. Connect: risk of extraneous from automated/unannotated links; strategy: annotated self-explanation linking. Retrieve: risk of passive re-reading; strategy: spaced retrieval practice.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** CLT, PKM, original-framework, load-topology

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Why does the retrieval practice effect produce superior memory consolidation compared to passive re-reading, in CLT terms?
> **Answer:** Retrieval practice generates a distinct pattern of cognitive reactivation: reconstructing information from memory requires schema activation and application, generating the moderate-difficulty germane processing that consolidates memory traces. Passive re-reading merely re-exposes the brain to the same input without requiring reactivation of the stored schema, producing recognition without consolidation. In CLT terms, retrieval practice generates productive germane load (schema reactivation and strengthening) while passive re-reading generates low-benefit processing that mimics encoding without the consolidation-generating cognitive effort.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** retrieval-practice, germane-load, PKM, spaced-repetition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is cognitive offloading, and when is it adaptive vs. maladaptive in PKM practice?
> **Answer:** Cognitive offloading is using external resources to perform cognitive operations that would otherwise be performed internally, reducing working memory demands. In PKM: adaptive when the offloaded operation (storing a reference, recording a fact for later retrieval) would impose excessive WM load without contributing to schema formation — the external storage frees WM for the schema-building processing that matters. Maladaptive when it substitutes for internal processing that produces durable schemas: using AI to summarize instead of reading and reformulating eliminates the germane processing that would have formed the schema.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** cognitive-offloading, PKM, schema-formation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the CLT-PKM Alignment Framework, and what are its four phases?
> **Answer:** An original two-dimensional framework (this report) integrating CLT effects with PKM workflow phases. Four phases: (1) Capture — risk: extraneous load from simultaneous tasks; mechanism: elaborative encoding through reformulation; strategy: selective capture + delayed processing. (2) Structure — risk: intrinsic load from high element-interactivity notes; mechanism: chunking through well-scoped note boundaries; strategy: atomicity. (3) Connect — risk: extraneous load from automated/unannotated links; mechanism: relational schema construction; strategy: annotated linking with self-explanation rationale. (4) Retrieve — risk: passive re-reading without consolidation; mechanism: schema strengthening through reactivation; strategy: spaced retrieval practice.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** CLT-PKM-alignment, original-framework, PKM

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What does the modality effect recommend for PKM note design, and what is its boundary condition?
> **Answer:** The modality effect recommends that PKM notes on complex, high-element-interactivity material should integrate verbal and visual representations (diagrams, concept maps, timelines) because verbal-visual integration distributes processing across two working memory subsystems (phonological loop + visuospatial sketchpad), effectively expanding available processing capacity. Boundary condition: the benefit holds only when both representations carry complementary (non-redundant) information. A diagram that merely illustrates what the adjacent text already states creates redundancy load rather than load-distribution benefit.
> **Source:** Sections 3.4, 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** modality-effect, dual-coding, PKM-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** What is Cognitive Load Theory?
> **A:** **Q:** What is Cognitive Load Theory, and what is its central claim?
> **A:** CLT is an instructional design theory arguing that learning is optimized when instructional procedures accommodate the limitations of human cognitive architecture — specifically, the limited capacity of working memory and the organizational role of schemas in long-term memory. Its central claim is that instructional design should manage cognitive load by reducing extraneous demands and directing processing toward schema construction.
> **Source:** Sections 1-2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #clt-definition, #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Three Types of Cognitive Load
> **A:** **Q:** Name and define the three types of cognitive load in CLT's original framework.
> **A:** (1) *Intrinsic load* — inherent complexity of the material determined by element interactivity relative to learner expertise; cannot be altered by design. (2) *Extraneous load* — unnecessary load imposed by poor instructional design; always detrimental and always reducible. (3) *Germane load* — working memory resources devoted to schema construction; later reconceptualized as not an independent source but a functional description of resources allocated to processing intrinsic load.
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #load-types, #intrinsic, #extraneous, #germane

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Element Interactivity
> **A:** **Q:** What is element interactivity, and why is it central to CLT?
> **A:** Element interactivity is the degree to which elements of a learning task must be processed simultaneously because they are logically interdependent. It is central because it determines intrinsic cognitive load: high interactivity means more elements must be held and integrated in working memory simultaneously. It is also expertise-relative — experts chunk elements via schemas, reducing effective interactivity.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #element-interactivity, #intrinsic-load, #mechanism

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Worked Example vs. Problem Solving
> **A:** **Q:** Why do worked examples produce better learning than problem-solving practice for novices, according to CLT?
> **A:** Conventional problem-solving requires means-ends analysis — simultaneously maintaining goal state, current state, operators, and subgoals — which imposes heavy extraneous load that does not contribute to schema construction. Worked examples eliminate means-ends analysis, allowing learners to devote working memory to understanding the solution rationale, which promotes schema construction.
> **Source:** Section 5.1
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #worked-example-effect, #means-ends-analysis, #schema-construction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Expertise Reversal Effect
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise reversal effect, and what is its key implication for instructional design?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect is the finding that instructional techniques effective for novices (e.g., worked examples, integrated formats) become ineffective or counterproductive for advanced learners. The key implication is that no single instructional approach is universally optimal — effective CLT-based design must be adaptive, adjusting support to learner expertise level.
> **Source:** Sections 4, 5.5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #expertise-reversal, #adaptive-instruction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Split Attention vs. Redundancy
> **A:** **Q:** What is the difference between the split attention effect and the redundancy effect?
> **A:** Split attention occurs when learners must mentally integrate two *mutually referring* sources that cannot be understood in isolation (solution: physically integrate them). Redundancy occurs when two sources are *self-contained* — each can be understood independently, so the additional source adds processing cost without information value (solution: remove the redundant source). The key distinction: mutually referring sources need integration; self-contained sources need elimination.
> **Source:** Sections 5.2, 5.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #split-attention, #redundancy, #design-principle

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Germane Load Reconceptualization
> **A:** **Q:** How did Sweller (2010) reconceptualize germane cognitive load, and why?
> **A:** Sweller argued that germane load is not an independent load source parallel to intrinsic and extraneous load, but rather the working memory resources devoted to processing intrinsic load when extraneous load has been minimized. The reason: germane load cannot be independently measured or manipulated apart from intrinsic load, making it unfalsifiable as a separate construct. The reconceptualized model has only two independent sources: intrinsic and extraneous.
> **Source:** Section 7.1
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #germane-load, #reconceptualization, #theoretical-debate

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** CLT and Desirable Difficulties
> **A:** **Q:** How is the apparent tension between CLT (reduce load) and desirable difficulties (add difficulty) resolved?
> **A:** Desirable difficulties increase *intrinsic* processing demands — they require deeper engagement with the material's inherent structure (e.g., retrieval practice forces schema reconstruction, interleaving forces discrimination). They do not add extraneous load. CLT predicts these should enhance learning when total load remains within working memory capacity, which the evidence confirms — with the caveat that desirable difficulties fail when intrinsic load is already high.
> **Source:** Section 7.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #desirable-difficulties, #reconciliation, #intrinsic-load

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Productive Failure
> **A:** **Q:** What is productive failure, and how does it challenge and extend CLT?
> **A:** Productive failure (Kapur) involves learners first attempting problems without instruction (and failing), then receiving direct instruction. It often outperforms instruction-first approaches. It challenges CLT's prediction that novices should receive guidance first, but extends the theory by demonstrating that pre-instruction failure activates prior knowledge, reveals gaps, and creates "knowledge differentiation" that makes subsequent instruction more effective. It suggests CLT needs temporal framing — optimizing across learning sequences, not just individual events.
> **Source:** Section 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #productive-failure, #boundary-condition, #sequence-effects

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> **Q:** Working Memory Capacity Estimates
> **A:** **Q:** What are the key estimates of working memory capacity relevant to CLT, and who proposed them?
> **A:** George Miller (1956) proposed "7 ± 2" items, but this includes rehearsed and chunked items. Nelson Cowan (2001) argued that *pure* working memory capacity — controlling for rehearsal and chunking — is approximately 4 chunks. CLT adopts the more conservative estimate, as it follows directly that high element interactivity (>4 novel interacting elements) will exceed capacity for novices.
> **Source:** Section 2.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory, #capacity, #miller, #cowan

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four components of Baddeley's Working Memory Model (most recent version)?
> **A:** (1) Phonological Loop (verbal/phonological storage and rehearsal); (2) Visuospatial Sketchpad (visual and spatial maintenance/manipulation); (3) Central Executive (attentional controller — updating, shifting, inhibiting); (4) Episodic Buffer (multimodal integrator with LTM, added 2000).
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** working-memory, baddeley-model, cognitive-architecture

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Distinguish Tulving's *episodic* from *semantic* memory. What is the key phenomenological marker Tulving used to define the distinction?
> **A:** *Episodic memory* stores personally experienced events in their spatiotemporal context, enabling "mental time travel" — the subjective re-experiencing of past events (autonoetic consciousness). *Semantic memory* stores general world knowledge without personal temporal context (noetic consciousness — knowing about, not remembering experiencing). Tulving used *autonoetic consciousness* (self-aware consciousness of one's personal past) as the defining phenomenological marker of episodic memory.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** long-term-memory, tulving, episodic-memory, semantic-memory, consciousness

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the misinformation effect (Loftus)? Give one example from the original research.
> **A:** The misinformation effect is the incorporation of post-event information (leading questions, misleading suggestions, others' accounts) into the memory of the original event, subsequently recalled as genuine. Example: participants shown a car accident video and asked "How fast were the cars going when they *smashed* into each other?" reported higher speeds and more often reported seeing broken glass than those asked about "contact" — though no broken glass was present.
> **Source:** Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** misinformation-effect, reconstructive-memory, elizabeth-loftus, eyewitness-testimony

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does "encoding specificity" predict about memory retrieval?
> **A:** The Encoding Specificity Principle (Tulving & Thomson, 1973) predicts that retrieval success is greatest when retrieval cues match the context present at encoding. The memory trace encodes both the target item and the encoding context (environmental cues, internal state, associated stimuli). Reinstating encoding context at retrieval facilitates recall; mismatching context impairs it.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** encoding-specificity, retrieval, tulving, context-dependent-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is reconsolidation, and why does it matter for both memory theory and clinical practice?
> **A:** Reconsolidation is the process by which a retrieved (reactivated) memory returns to a brief labile state (protein-synthesis dependent) before being re-stabilized. It challenges the once-consolidated-always-stable view of LTM and provides a cellular mechanism for memory updating. Clinically: it suggests that reactivating traumatic memories in safe contexts with new, non-fear-associated information may allow the fear-associated elements to be updated — a basis for reconsolidation-based exposure therapies and pharmacological interventions (e.g., propranolol) in PTSD.
> **Source:** Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** reconsolidation, memory-consolidation, PTSD, clinical-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Compare Cowan's and Miller's capacity estimates for working memory. What explains the difference?
> **A:** Miller (1956) estimated short-term memory capacity at 7 ± 2 *chunks*. Cowan (2001) re-analyzed the literature and argued the true capacity of the *focus of attention* is approximately 4 ± 1 *chunks*. The difference is explained by the contribution of the phonological rehearsal loop: Miller's higher estimates partially reflected phonological rehearsal support (cycling items through the inner voice), which inflates apparent visual or attentional capacity. Cowan's estimate isolates the central attentional capacity without rehearsal's contribution.
> **Source:** Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** working-memory-capacity, miller, cowan, chunks, phonological-loop

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are "desirable difficulties" (Bjork)? Give three examples and explain why they improve learning despite feeling harder.
> **A:** Desirable difficulties are learning conditions that slow or impair *acquisition performance* while substantially improving *long-term retention and transfer*. Three examples: (1) *Spaced practice:* distributing study over time rather than massing it; (2) *Interleaving:* mixing problem types rather than blocking by type; (3) *Retrieval practice:* testing recall rather than re-reading. They improve learning because: effortful retrieval strengthens the memory trace more than fluent access; spacing allows partial forgetting that makes each review a genuine retrieval challenge (triggering reconsolidation); interleaving requires discrimination learning between problem types, building more flexible schemas.
> **Source:** Section 5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** desirable-difficulties, spacing-effect, testing-effect, retrieval-practice, bjork

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "expertise reversal effect" in Cognitive Load Theory, and what does it imply about instructional design?
> **A:** The expertise reversal effect (Sweller): instructional techniques effective for novices become redundant or even detrimental for experts. Specifically, the worked example effect — novices learn better from worked examples than from unguided problem-solving — reverses for experts, who are better served by problem-solving that extends their schemas. The implication: instructional design must be adaptive to learner expertise level; a single fixed instructional format will be suboptimal for at least one expertise level. Novices need scaffolded examples to reduce [[element-interactivity|element interactivity]]; experts need problems to challenge their existing schemas.
> **Source:** Section 8
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** cognitive-load-theory, expertise-reversal, worked-examples, instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What role does the amygdala play in memory, and how does this explain the enhanced memorability of emotional events?
> **A:** The amygdala modulates memory consolidation for emotionally significant events by enhancing hippocampal consolidation during high-arousal states. Stress hormones (norepinephrine, cortisol) released during emotional experiences act on the amygdala, which in turn strengthens hippocampal long-term potentiation during post-encoding consolidation. This produces the well-documented emotional memory enhancement — emotionally arousing events are remembered more vividly and durably than neutral events of equal objective importance — and also the enhanced intrusiveness and traumatic re-experiencing that characterize fear memory in PTSD.
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** amygdala, emotional-memory, hippocampus, PTSD, memory-consolidation

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does Cognitive Load Theory identify as the primary constraint on learning?
> **A:** The limited capacity of working memory. Learning requires processing new information through working memory, which can hold approximately 4 independent chunks simultaneously. Instruction that overloads working memory prevents the schema formation that constitutes lasting learning.
> **Source:** Section 2 — CLT Foundations
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #clt #working-memory #cognitive-architecture

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the additivity constraint in Cognitive Load Theory?
> **A:** The principle that intrinsic, extraneous, and germane loads sum to a total cognitive load that must not exceed working memory capacity. A design implication: if intrinsic load is high (complex material), extraneous load reduction becomes especially critical, as the capacity available for productive engagement is already substantially consumed.
> **Source:** Section 3 — Architecture of Cognitive Load
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #clt #intrinsic-load #working-memory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes intrinsic from extraneous cognitive load?
> **A:** Intrinsic load arises from the inherent complexity of the material (element interactivity) and cannot be reduced without changing the material itself. Extraneous load arises from how the material is presented or organised and is always potentially reducible through better design. The diagnostic question: is the difficulty from the topic or from the structure?
> **Source:** Section 3 — Architecture of Cognitive Load
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #clt #intrinsic-load #extraneous-load #distinction

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the expertise-reversal effect and why does it matter for PKM?
> **A:** The expertise-reversal effect is the phenomenon whereby instructional supports effective for novices (worked examples, detailed scaffolding, explicit procedures) become ineffective or counterproductive for experts. For PKM, it implies that optimal system design is expertise-relative: highly structured, prescriptive frameworks suit beginners; flexible, sparse systems with rich semantic linking suit advanced practitioners. There is no universally optimal PKM design.
> **Source:** Section 8 — Expertise-Reversal Effect
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #expertise-reversal #pkm-design #adaptive-systems

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is synthesis debt and why does it generate extraneous load?
> **A:** Synthesis debt is the accumulated backlog of captured but unprocessed information in a PKM system. It generates extraneous load primarily through incomplete-task intrusion: unclosed cognitive loops around material that has been stored but not evaluated. Additionally, future synthesis becomes more costly as context recedes — re-contextualising notes captured weeks or months ago requires more effort than immediate processing.
> **Source:** Section 5 — How PKM Systems Fail
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #synthesis-debt #extraneous-load #pkm-failure-modes

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the episodic buffer and why is it significant for PKM?
> **A:** The episodic buffer is the fourth component of Baddeley's working memory model — a limited-capacity, multi-modal workspace that integrates information from the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and long-term memory into coherent episodes. It is significant for PKM because synthesis tasks — integrating information from multiple notes, connecting new material to existing schemas — appear to engage the episodic buffer as their primary workspace. PKM activities that preserve the multi-modal, integrated representations the episodic buffer generates are most supportive of synthesis.
> **Source:** Section 4 — Working Memory Architecture
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #episodic-buffer #working-memory #pkm-synthesis

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect and how should it inform PKM practice?
> **A:** The generation effect is the robust finding that self-generated items (solutions, examples, connects) are better retained than items received in completed form. For PKM practice, it implies that generating your own examples, questions, and summaries when processing notes produces stronger encoding than passively annotating or highlighting. This is a core rationale for write-from-memory protocols.
> **Source:** Section 7 — Germane Load and Schema Construction
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #generation-effect #elaborative-encoding #pkm-practice

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Cognitive Priority Principle?
> **A:** The Cognitive Priority Principle (proposed in this report) holds that PKM practitioners should allocate cognitive resources proportionally to their cognitive value. Synthesis and connection generation — the highest-value activities — should receive the most protected, focused cognitive effort. Capture and filing — the lowest-value activities — should be made as automatic and effortless as possible. The error is allocating to the PKM system's maintenance precisely the concentrated, deliberate effort that should be directed to synthesis.
> **Source:** Synthesis section — Cognitive Priority Principle
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #cognitive-priority-principle #pkm-design #original-synthesis

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does research on retrieval practice imply for PKM design?
> **A:** Karpicke & Roediger (2008) demonstrated that a single retrieval test produces better long-term retention than four re-studying sessions. For PKM design, this implies that the dominant activity in a PKM review cycle should be active recall (generating content without looking at notes) rather than passive re-reading. Systems that only support capture and browsing, without building in retrieval practice mechanisms, fail to leverage the most effective consolidation strategy available.
> **Source:** Section 7 — Germane Load and Schema Construction
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #retrieval-practice #spaced-repetition #pkm-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three conditions under which a PKM system functions as cognitive infrastructure rather than cognitive overhead?
> **A:** (1) Access costs are lower than reconstruction costs for stored material — the system reliably surfaces relevant content in less time and cognitive effort than would be needed to reconstruct it internally. (2) Maintenance costs remain low enough that they do not consume working memory resources needed for synthesis — the system is largely self-maintaining or requires minimal ongoing curation. (3) The system actively promotes germane processing through its structural logic — it requires or rewards connection-making, retrieval, and elaboration rather than only rewarding accumulation.
> **Source:** Synthesis section
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #cognitive-infrastructure #pkm-design #cognitive-conditions

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-and-cognitive-load-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is element interactivity and how does it determine intrinsic load?
> **A:** Element interactivity is the degree to which understanding a body of information requires simultaneously processing multiple interdependent elements. High element interactivity = high intrinsic load. Knowledge management tasks with high element interactivity include: synthesising across multiple perspectives with conflicting claims, building models that integrate multiple constraints, and generating novel connections across domains. Tasks with low element interactivity include: tagging, filing, and labelling — but these generate extraneous rather than intrinsic load.
> **Source:** Section 3 and Section 6
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #element-interactivity #intrinsic-load #knowledge-work

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is working memory and how does it differ from short-term memory?
> **Answer:** Working memory is a multi-component system for the active storage and manipulation of information during ongoing tasks — not merely passive temporary storage. Short-term memory describes temporary retention; working memory describes active processing. The key difference is the central executive's role in manipulating, not merely holding, information.
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** working-memory, cognitive-architecture, distinction
> **Source:** Section 1 — Working Memory Architecture

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Name the three types of cognitive offloading in Risko & Gilbert's taxonomy and provide an example of each.
> **Answer:** (1) Motor offloading — using bodily action to reduce internal processing, e.g., counting on fingers; (2) Perceptual offloading — using environmental features to reduce pattern recognition, e.g., using a map rather than maintaining spatial information internally; (3) Computational offloading — externalising calculation or organisation, e.g., writing a calculation rather than performing it mentally.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** cognitive-offloading, taxonomy, memory
> **Source:** Section 2 — Cognitive Offloading Taxonomy

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the Parity Principle (Clark & Chalmers) and why does it matter for PKM?
> **Answer:** The Parity Principle states: if an external component plays the same functional role that an internal component plays — and satisfies the coupling conditions — it counts as part of the cognitive system. For PKM, this implies a well-designed, trusted PKB is not merely a tool but a genuine constituent of the practitioner's cognitive apparatus.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** extended-mind, philosophy-of-mind, PKM
> **Source:** Section 3 — Extended Mind Theory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What are the three coupling conditions for genuine cognitive extension in the Clark-Chalmers framework?
> **Answer:** (1) Reliable functionality — the resource must be consistently and trustworthily available; (2) Direct accessibility — information must be retrievable directly without complex verification; (3) Past endorsement — the content must have been previously endorsed by the cognitive agent and is assumed correct. All three must be jointly satisfied.
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** extended-mind, coupling-conditions, PKM-design
> **Source:** Section 3 — Extended Mind Theory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Explain the Substitution Trap in PKM contexts.
> **Answer:** The Substitution Trap occurs when cognitive offloading replaces (rather than supplements) the internal processing that contributes to [[long-term-memory]] consolidation. Capturing an idea into a PKB inbox without generative reformulation offloads working memory but bypasses schema formation — the information is relocated without being learned. Result: growing archive → reduced learning → PKB never becomes a genuine extended cognitive resource.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** substitution-trap, cognitive-offloading, generative-processing
> **Source:** Section 2 — Substitution Trap

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is an epistemic action and how does the PKB function as an epistemic action infrastructure?
> **Answer:** An epistemic action (Kirsh & Maglio, 1994) is an action taken not to advance physical task completion but to simplify internal cognition by restructuring environmental information. The PKB functions as an epistemic action infrastructure when it performs cognitive operations (surfacing connections, scheduling review, generating unexpected juxtapositions) on behalf of the practitioner — not merely storing data but actively computing with it.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** epistemic-action, extended-mind, PKM-design, retrieval-as-computation
> **Source:** Section 3 + Retrieval-as-Computation Principle

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What distinguishes germane load from extraneous load in Cognitive Load Theory?
> **Answer:** Germane load is cognitive effort that directly contributes to schema formation and long-term memory consolidation — it is the "good" load of learning. Extraneous load is cognitive effort imposed by poor design that consumes working memory without contributing to schema formation — it is waste. PKM design should systematically eliminate extraneous load (unnecessary friction, organisational overhead) and protect germane processing (reformulation, connection-drawing, integration).
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** cognitive-load-theory, PKM-design, germane-load
> **Source:** Section 1 + Section 5 Principle 1

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** What is the Cognitive Economy Model of PKM and what prediction does it make?
> **Answer:** The Cognitive Economy Model (introduced in this report) characterises PKM as a temporal investment: early stages require net cognitive investment (capture + processing overhead) while downstream stages yield compound cognitive returns (offloaded maintenance, enhanced retrieval). The model predicts that the PKM benefit is front-loaded in cost and back-loaded in return, explaining why many practitioners abandon PKM before reaching the compounding phase and mistakenly conclude it does not work.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** cognitive-economy, PKM, long-term-investment
> **Source:** Section 4 — Cognitive Economy Model

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Why is the integration stage of the Capture → Process → Integrate pipeline the most cognitively important, and why is it most frequently neglected?
> **Answer:** Integration is most cognitively important because it builds the relational structure that enables the PKB to function as an epistemic action infrastructure — it converts isolated notes into a semantic network that performs computational work through connections. It is most neglected because it is cognitively demanding (requires navigating existing structure), produces no immediate visible output (unlike capture or processing), and its benefits are delayed and non-obvious until the PKB reaches critical connectivity.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** integration, PKM-workflow, epistemic-action-infrastructure
> **Source:** Section 6 — The Integration Bottleneck

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[pkm-working-memory-cognitive-offloading-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Question:** Name and briefly explain three [[desirable-difficulties]] from the learning science literature and explain why each matters for PKM review design.
> **Answer:** (1) Spacing Effect — distributed practice is more effective than massed practice for long-term retention → PKM review should be spaced, not reviewed all at once; (2) Testing Effect — retrieval practice (actively recalling) is more effective than re-reading → PKM review should require active recall, not passive re-reading of notes; (3) Generation Effect — self-generated material is better consolidated than passively received material → PKM note-making should involve reformulation in own words, not verbatim capture.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** desirable-difficulties, spaced-repetition, generative-processing, PKM-review
> **Source:** Section 2 + Section 5 Generative Investment Principle

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the key distinction between cognitive offloading and schema externalization?
> **A:** Cognitive offloading reduces working memory burden by moving storage outside the head; schema externalization targets the organizational structure of external systems to mirror the relational, hierarchical, and associative properties of schematic long-term memory. Offloading is about capacity; externalization is about architecture.
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Source:** Section 1: Definitions and Scope
> **Tags:** `schema-externalization` `cognitive-offloading` `PKB-design`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Fluency Illusion and why is it specifically dangerous for PKB practitioners?
> **A:** The fluency illusion is the tendency to interpret ease of processing familiar material as evidence of durable understanding, when actually the ease reflects only surface familiarity. For PKB practitioners: the experience of easily navigating a well-organized knowledge base feels like understanding, but ease of navigation measures the PKB's organizational clarity, not the practitioner's internal schema development. A practitioner can have a perfectly organized PKB and weak internal schemas simultaneously.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 8: Risks, Tensions, and the Fluency Trap
> **Tags:** `fluency-illusion` `metacognition` `PKB-risks` `schema-development`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What are the five structural properties of a high schema-fidelity PKB?
> **A:** (1) Relational primacy — links are typed and semantically specified, not just navigation paths; (2) Hierarchical stratification — schema nodes (MOCs) sit above linked detail notes; (3) Associative density — conceptual neighborhoods form through multiple overlapping links; (4) Categorical coherence — notes within clusters share genuine underlying category membership; (5) Activation pathway design — from any note, related concepts are fast to reach, mimicking spreading activation.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 6: The PKB as Externalized Schema Network
> **Tags:** `schema-fidelity` `PKB-architecture` `knowledge-organization`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is spreading activation and why does it matter for PKB design?
> **A:** Spreading activation is the mechanism by which activating one concept node in associative memory automatically propagates along weighted links to neighboring nodes, partially activating them. It operates pre-consciously and enables expert fluency. It matters for PKB design because a knowledge graph with richly typed, dense links creates a structural substrate that partially externalizes the activation pathway network — though the practitioner must manually traverse it, the structure facilitates the same conceptual neighborhood access that spreading activation provides internally.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Section 3: Schema-Driven Processing
> **Tags:** `spreading-activation` `PKB-architecture` `semantic-memory`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Bidirectional Enrichment Hypothesis?
> **A:** The BiEH proposes a compounding feedback cycle between PKB use (external) and internal schema development: (Outside-in) Building skeleton frameworks, elaborating links, and writing schemas for the PKB requires self-explanation, which strengthens internal schema organization. (Inside-out) Richer internal schemas enable more structurally accurate and associatively dense PKB construction. Both directions feed each other, generating compounding returns for practitioners who maintain the cycle.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Section 7: Bidirectional Enrichment
> **Tags:** `bidirectional-enrichment` `PKB-theory` `schema-development` `original-synthesis`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Describe the Parity Principle (Clark & Chalmers) and its relevance to PKB design.
> **A:** The Parity Principle: if an external process plays the same functional role that an internal cognitive process would play, it is rightfully classified as part of the cognitive system. Relevance to PKB: if a PKB participates in schema-driven processing in the same functional role that schematic LTM would, it qualifies as cognitive extension. However, the principle requires the use conditions to be met (reliable availability, accessible endorsement, habitually trusted); the schema externalization thesis doesn't require the full metaphysical claim, only that structural correspondence improves coupling quality and functional contribution.
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Section 5: The Extended Mind Thesis
> **Tags:** `parity-principle` `extended-mind` `cognitive-extension` `Andy-Clark`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes intrinsic, extraneous, and germane cognitive load?
> **A:** Intrinsic load: inherent complexity of the material, determined by element interactivity — cannot be reduced except by building schemas first. Extraneous load: unnecessary cognitive work from poor presentation design — should be minimized. Germane load: cognitive effort directed at schema construction and elaboration — should be optimized (not maximized). A PKB supports learning by reducing extraneous load (outsourcing reference material) and freeing capacity for germane load (active schema building), NOT by reducing intrinsic load, which remains constant.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 4: Working Memory as the Bottleneck
> **Tags:** `cognitive-load` `CLT` `working-memory` `schema-construction`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What is the Three-Register PKB framework?
> **A:** The Three-Register PKB proposes that an effective schema-fidelity PKB serves three functional registers simultaneously: (1) Foundation — permanent notes capturing concept-level schema nodes for long-term stability; (2) Synthesis — MOCs and hub notes articulating relationships and hierarchical structure among schema nodes; (3) Laboratory — ephemeral working notes, active reading annotations, and analytical drafts capturing the current knowledge-work process. The three registers correspond to semantic memory (stable concepts), schema structure (relations and hierarchies), and working memory support (active processing).
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Source:** Synthesis and Integration
> **Tags:** `three-register-PKB` `PKB-architecture` `original-synthesis`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** Why does the encoding specificity principle create a challenge for PKB practitioners?
> **A:** Encoding specificity (Tulving & Thomson): retrieval is most effective when retrieval cues match the encoding context. The challenge: when we later search the PKB from a different conceptual context than the one in which we created a note, retrieval may fail even when the note is relevant — because the retrieval cue doesn't match the conceptual context embedded in the note's creation. Schema-fidelity design addresses this by creating multiple access pathways to each concept (multiple links from different contexts), reducing dependence on any single encoding context for retrieval.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 3: Schema-Driven Processing
> **Tags:** `encoding-specificity` `retrieval` `PKB-design` `Tulving`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[schema-externalization-pkb-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-05]])*
> **Q:** Untitled
> **A:** **Q:** What does expertise research (Chase & Simon, 1973) reveal about chunking and schemas?
> **A:** Chase & Simon's chessboard studies showed that expert chess players can reconstruct 20+ piece positions from 5-second viewing while novices reconstruct only 4–6 pieces. The advantage disappears with randomly arranged pieces. This reveals that experts don't have more working memory capacity — they have richer schemas: they perceive meaningful configurations (attacks, defenses, standard positions) as single chunks, each representing 5–7 individual pieces. The key insight for PKBs: chunking multiplies information density per working memory slot, and chunking capacity is entirely dependent on schema richness.
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Source:** Section 2: The Architecture of Schemas in Long-Term Memory
> **Tags:** `chunking` `expertise` `schemas` `working-memory` `Chase-and-Simon`

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 1
> **A:** **Q:** What are the four components of Baddeley's working memory model?
> **A:** The phonological loop (verbal/acoustic storage), visuospatial sketchpad (visual/spatial processing), episodic buffer (integration workspace), and central executive (attentional control and coordination).
> **Source:** Section 1.1
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory #cognitive-architecture

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 2
> **A:** **Q:** What is the current best estimate for working memory capacity in the focus of attention?
> **A:** Approximately 3-5 items, with 4 as the modal estimate (Cowan, 2001) — significantly lower than Miller's classic 7±2.
> **Source:** Section 1.2
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory #capacity-limits

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 3
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three types of cognitive load in Sweller's Cognitive Load Theory, and how do they compete?
> **A:** Intrinsic load (from material complexity/element interactivity), extraneous load (from poor design or environment), and germane load (productive schema-building effort). They are additive and cannot exceed total WM capacity — any increase in extraneous load subtracts from germane processing.
> **Source:** Section 2.1–2.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #cognitive-load-theory #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 4
> **A:** **Q:** What does Wickens' Multiple Resource Theory predict about walking while reading?
> **A:** Relatively LOW interference, because walking is primarily motor-spatial-ambient and reading is primarily cognitive-verbal-focal. They overlap mainly on central executive coordination, but use largely distinct resource pools.
> **Source:** Section 3.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #multiple-resource-theory #dual-task

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 5
> **A:** **Q:** What key finding from Oppezzo & Schwartz (2014) is commonly misinterpreted regarding walking and cognition?
> **A:** Walking enhanced *divergent* (creative) thinking by ~60%, but it slightly *impaired* convergent (analytical) thinking. Reading comprehension is convergent — so the study actually argues against walking while reading complex analytical material.
> **Source:** Section 5.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #embodied-cognition #creativity #convergent-vs-divergent

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 6
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "timing asymmetry" in the exercise-cognition literature, and what does it imply for reading?
> **A:** Exercise during complex cognitive tasks shows null-to-negative effects, while exercise before complex tasks shows reliably positive effects (lasting 30-60 minutes post-exercise). Implication: walk BEFORE reading, not during.
> **Source:** Section 6.2
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #exercise-cognition #yerkes-dodson

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 7
> **A:** **Q:** What is the generation effect, and why does it make the walking-reading question practically irrelevant?
> **A:** The generation effect (Slamecka & Graf, 1978) shows that actively producing information yields d = 0.40–0.87 better retention than passive reception. Walking-reading is inherently passive (no annotation, summarization, or PKB processing possible). The opportunity cost of losing the generation effect (~40-80% retention benefit) vastly exceeds walking's modest dual-task cost (~10-25%).
> **Source:** Section 7.1, 7.3
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #generation-effect #opportunity-cost #active-learning

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 8
> **A:** **Q:** What are the three conditions under which walking-reading remains defensible according to this report?
> **A:** (1) Material you already know well (low intrinsic load due to developed schemas), (2) low element-interactivity material (narrative, biographical), and (3) audio-first formats (eliminates gaze-stabilization and device-management costs).
> **Source:** Section 8.5
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #practical-strategy #walking-reading

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 9
> **A:** **Q:** Why is automaticity better understood as a continuum than a binary, and what does this mean for walking while reading?
> **A:** Moors & De Houwer (2006) argue that automaticity features (efficiency, awareness, intentionality, controllability) are partially independent — a process can be automatic on some features but not others. Walking is efficient and low-awareness but requires ongoing balance monitoring, turning decisions, and gaze stabilization. These residual demands fall on a working memory system with no spare capacity during complex reading.
> **Source:** Section 4.1–4.2
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #automaticity #graded-automaticity #dual-task

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[walking-and-academic-reading-foundational-report-2026-04-13]])*
> **Q:** SR Seed 10
> **A:** **Q:** What is the "Walk-Read-Walk Sandwich" protocol, and what cognitive mechanism does each phase exploit?
> **A:** (1) Pre-walk (15-20 min): schema activation, arousal priming via exercise-cognition effects. (2) Desk reading (45-60 min): deep comprehension and generation effect through active PKB engagement. (3) Post-walk (15-20 min): memory consolidation, retrieval practice, and creative incubation through embodied cognition effects.
> **Source:** Section 8.2–8.3
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #practical-strategy #study-protocol

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Working Memory
> **A:** **Q:** What distinguishes working memory from short-term memory?
> **A:** Working memory includes active manipulation and attentional control processes (via the central executive), not just passive temporary storage.
> **Source:** Section 1, Section 3
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #working-memory, #definition, #cognitive-psychology

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Definition: Element Interactivity
> **A:** **Q:** What is element interactivity, and why is it learner-relative?
> **A:** Element interactivity is the number of information elements that must be processed simultaneously. It is learner-relative because expertise enables chunking: elements that interact for a novice may be integrated into a single schema for an expert.
> **Source:** Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #cognitive-load-theory, #element-interactivity, #definition

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Multicomponent Model vs. Embedded-Processes Model
> **A:** **Q:** What is the fundamental architectural difference between Baddeley's and Cowan's models?
> **A:** Baddeley posits dedicated, modality-specific subsystems (phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad) separate from LTM. Cowan posits that working memory IS the activated portion of LTM plus an attentional focus.
> **Source:** Section 3, Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #working-memory, #distinction, #theoretical-models

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Intrinsic vs. Extraneous Cognitive Load
> **A:** **Q:** How do intrinsic and extraneous cognitive load differ in their relationship to instructional design?
> **A:** Intrinsic load is determined by element interactivity and cannot be reduced without changing what is taught. Extraneous load is caused by poor instructional design and can be eliminated without changing content.
> **Source:** Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #cognitive-load-theory, #distinction, #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Process: Schema Automation
> **A:** **Q:** How does schema automation affect working memory capacity?
> **A:** Automated schemas can be executed with minimal working memory demand, freeing capacity for higher-order processing. The schema still operates but no longer competes for the ~4-item capacity limit.
> **Source:** Section 5, Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #schema-automation, #process, #expertise

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Application: Modality Effect
> **A:** **Q:** Why does presenting verbal information as narration (auditory) rather than on-screen text (visual) improve learning from diagrams?
> **A:** Because narration uses the phonological loop while the diagram uses the visuospatial sketchpad, distributing load across two subsystems rather than overloading the visual channel alone.
> **Source:** Section 3, Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Intermediate
> **Tags:** #modality-effect, #application, #instructional-design

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Connection: Working Memory and Metacognition
> **A:** **Q:** What is the metacognitive overhead problem?
> **A:** Metacognitive monitoring and regulation are themselves working-memory-intensive operations. Novices, who most need metacognitive support, have the least working memory available for it because their lack of automated schemas means processing already consumes most capacity.
> **Source:** Section 8
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #metacognition, #working-memory, #connection, #original-synthesis

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Connection: Capacity Limits and Learning Design
> **A:** **Q:** Why is the ~4-item capacity limit the most consequential finding in working memory research for educational practice?
> **A:** Because it sets the absolute upper bound on how much new information a learner can process simultaneously, making it the constraint that all instructional design must accommodate.
> **Source:** Section 5, Section 7
> **Difficulty:** Basic
> **Tags:** #capacity-limits, #connection, #cognitive-load-theory

> [!flashcard] **Spaced Repetition** *(from [[working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-16]])*
> **Q:** Distinction: Focus of Attention vs. Direct-Access Region (Oberauer)
> **A:** **Q:** What distinction does Oberauer's concentric model make within working memory?
> **A:** Oberauer distinguishes (1) the single-item focus of attention (most active), (2) a direct-access region of ~3-4 items (immediately available), and (3) activated LTM (accessible but requiring retrieval).
> **Source:** Section 4
> **Difficulty:** Advanced
> **Tags:** #working-memory, #distinction, #theoretical-models




































































































## Key Figures & Intellectual Lineage

> [!person] **Alan D. Baddeley (1934– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Alan Baddeley** is the founder and primary architect of the multicomponent working memory model. His 2000 review in *Nature Reviews Neuroscience* remains one of the most cited papers in cognitive psychology. Baddeley's work spans the entire developmental arc of the model from 1974 through the 2000 episodic buffer addition and subsequent empirical work. He served as Director of the Medical Research Council's Applied Psychology Unit (APU) in Cambridge, which produced many of the key empirical studies supporting the model. His broader research programme addresses amnesia, prospective memory, and the relationship between working memory and long-term memory consolidation.

> [!person] **Graham Hitch (1945– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Graham Hitch** co-developed the multicomponent model with Baddeley in the landmark 1974 paper. His sustained contributions to the empirical investigation of working memory — particularly the phonological loop's role in cognitive development and mathematical cognition — extended the model's scope across the lifespan. His later work with Christopher Jarrold on working memory and developmental disability has influenced understanding of intellectual developmental disorders.

> [!person] **Nelson Cowan (1951– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Nelson Cowan** developed the embedded-processes model of working memory as the primary theoretical alternative to Baddeley's multicomponent framework. His 2001 capacity estimate of ~4 chunks in the focus of attention has become a widely cited marker of working memory's fundamental limit. His work on the development of working memory in childhood (alongside colleagues including Candice Morey and Christopher Jarrold) has been particularly influential on developmental cognitive psychology.

> [!person] **John Sweller** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **John Sweller** developed [[cognitive-load-theory]] explicitly from Baddeley's working memory architecture, first articulating the theory in his 1988 paper on cognitive load during problem-solving. His programme of research identified the split-attention effect, the worked-example effect, the modality effect, and many other instructional phenomena predicted by CLT, and his collaborations with Fred Paas, Paul Chandler, and others produced the extensive meta-analytic evidence base that CLT now rests on.

> [!person] **Richard Mayer (1947– )** *(from [[baddeley-model-of-working-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-03]])*
> **Richard Mayer** developed the [[cognitive-theory-of-multimedia-learning]] in parallel with CLT, drawing on Baddeley's dual-channel architecture to produce twelve multimedia design principles supported by a large body of controlled experimental evidence. His textbook *Multimedia Learning* (2001; 2nd ed. 2009) is one of the most widely used educational psychology references internationally. His research on the modality effect, coherence principle, and segmenting principle has directly informed e-learning design worldwide.

> [!person] **George A. Miller (1920–2012)** — Harvard University, Princeton University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Foundational contributor to the cognitive revolution and the study of memory capacity. Miller's 1956 paper "The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two" established the concept of chunking and the limited capacity of short-term memory, providing the intellectual starting point for the entire research tradition examined in this report. His work bridged [[Information-Theory|information theory]] and experimental psychology, demonstrating that human information processing could be analysed in terms of channel capacity and recoding.
> **Key works referenced:** Miller (1956). *The Magical Number Seven, Plus or Minus Two.*
> **Relationships:** Influenced Simon, Newell, and virtually all subsequent working memory and expertise researchers.

> [!person] **Herbert A. Simon (1916–2001)** — Carnegie Mellon University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Nobel laureate in economics, pioneer of artificial intelligence, and central figure in the study of expertise and bounded rationality. With William Chase, Simon conducted the landmark chess expertise studies that demonstrated the chunk-based nature of expert memory. With Fernand Gobet, he developed template theory. Simon's estimate of 50,000–100,000 chunks in expert memory and the ten-year rule of expertise remain foundational reference points.
> **Key works referenced:** Chase & Simon (1973); Simon & Gobet (1996).
> **Relationships:** Extended Miller's chunking concept into expertise research; influenced Ericsson, Sweller, and the entire expert-novice research paradigm.

> [!person] **K. Anders Ericsson (1947–2020)** — Florida State University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the deliberate practice framework that has dominated expertise research since the 1990s. Ericsson argued that expert performance is primarily the product of extensive, targeted practice rather than innate talent, and that the mechanisms of expertise include the development of long-term working memory (LTWM) — domain-specific retrieval structures that allow experts to circumvent normal working memory limitations.
> **Key works referenced:** Ericsson, Krampe, & Tesch-Römer (1993); Ericsson & Pool (2016).
> **Relationships:** Built on Simon's chunk-based account of expertise; debated Hambrick and Macnamara on the practice-versus-talent question.

> [!person] **John Sweller (1946–)** — University of New South Wales** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed [[cognitive-load-theory|cognitive load theory]], the most influential framework for translating the cognitive science of chunking and schema development into instructional design principles. Sweller's work on intrinsic, extraneous, and germane cognitive load provided the theoretical architecture for understanding how instruction should be calibrated to the learner's developing expertise.
> **Key works referenced:** Sweller (1988); Sweller, Ayres, & Kalyuga (2011).
> **Relationships:** Drew directly on Miller's capacity limits and Simon's chunking work; influenced van Merriënboer, Kalyuga, Paas.

> [!person] **Alan D. Baddeley (1934–)** — University of York** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the multicomponent model of [[working-memory|working memory]] (with Graham Hitch), which replaced the unitary short-term store of the Atkinson-Shiffrin model with a complex system comprising a central executive, phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and episodic buffer. Baddeley's model provided the architectural framework within which chunking operates and helped clarify the distinction between passive storage and active manipulation.
> **Key works referenced:** Baddeley & Hitch (1974); Baddeley (2000).
> **Relationships:** Refined the working memory architecture within which Miller's chunking concept and Sweller's load theory operate.

> [!person] **Michelene T. H. Chi** — Arizona State University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Pioneered the study of expert-novice differences through her landmark studies of physics problem categorization, demonstrating that experts organize knowledge around deep structural principles while novices organize around surface features. This finding provided direct evidence that expertise involves the reorganization (not merely the accumulation) of domain knowledge.
> **Key works referenced:** Chi, Feltovich, & Glaser (1981).
> **Relationships:** Extended the expert-novice paradigm established by de Groot and Chase & Simon into the domain of problem categorization and conceptual understanding.

> [!person] **Gary Klein (1944–)** — Klein Associates** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the [[Recognition-Primed-Decision-Model|Recognition-Primed Decision]] model through studies of naturalistic expert decision making in high-stakes, time-pressured environments. Klein's work demonstrated that expert decisions are typically recognition-based rather than analytically comparative, providing the naturalistic decision-making complement to laboratory-based expertise research.
> **Key works referenced:** Klein (1998). *Sources of Power.*
> **Relationships:** Applied chunk-based models of expertise to real-world decision making; debated Kahneman on the conditions under which intuitive expertise is trustworthy.

> [!person] **Jeroen J. G. van Merriënboer** — Maastricht University** *(from [[chunking-and-expertise-development-foundational-report-2026-04-15]])*
> Developed the [[Four-Component-Instructional-Design|Four-Component Instructional Design]] (4C/ID) model, a comprehensive framework for designing instruction for complex learning tasks. The 4C/ID model translates the cognitive science of chunking, schema construction, and automation into a systematic instructional design methodology.
> **Key works referenced:** van Merriënboer & Kirschner (2018). *Ten Steps to Complex Learning.*
> **Relationships:** Built on Sweller's cognitive load theory; extended instructional design from simple to complex learning.

> [!person] **John Sweller (1946–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Emeritus Professor, University of New South Wales, Australia. Developed Cognitive Load Theory beginning in 1988, originating the framework from research on problem-solving and the means-ends analysis strategy. Sweller's evolutionary psychology framing (biologically primary vs. secondary knowledge) provided CLT with its theoretical unity beyond empirical findings. His 2010 reconceptualization of germane load represented significant self-correction. CLT's application to instructional design is his primary legacy.

> [!person] **Fred Paas (1962–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Professor, Erasmus University Rotterdam. Developed the Paas Cognitive Load Scale (1992), the most widely used measure of subjective mental effort in CLT research. Extensive work on self-assessment in learning, worked examples, and testing effects. Key collaborator with Sweller and van Merriënboer on the foundational CLT literature.

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer (1959–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Professor, Maastricht University. Co-developer with Sweller and Paas of the CLT theoretical framework. Primary originator of the 4C/ID model (Four Component Instructional Design), the most systematic translation of CLT into practical instructional design methodology. Extensive work on complex learning, task authenticity, and whole-task practice.

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley (1934–)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Emeritus Professor, University of York, UK. Developed the multi-component model of working memory (1974, with Graham Hitch), which underwent major elaborations in 2000 (adding the episodic buffer). Baddeley's model is the working memory architecture that CLT implicitly adopts, providing the empirical basis for the phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, and central executive distinctions that inform multimodal instructional design.

> [!person] **Niklas Luhmann (1927–1998)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-and-pkm-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> German sociologist and intellectual of extraordinary scope. Developed the Zettelkasten note-making method over a 40-year academic career producing over 70 books and 400 articles. Though not a learning scientist, Luhmann anticipated several CLT insights through his methodological practice: atomicity, explicit link annotation, distributed network rather than hierarchical organization. Luhmann's Zettelkasten is the empirical existence proof that an externalized associative network can amplify intellectual productivity in ways consistent with CLT's schema-formation requirements.

> [!person] **John Sweller (b. 1946) — University of New South Wales** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Creator of Cognitive Load Theory. Sweller's foundational contribution was demonstrating experimentally that conventional problem-solving practice imposes higher cognitive load and produces less learning than studying worked examples (1985, 1988). He subsequently developed the theoretical framework linking cognitive architecture to instructional design, introduced element interactivity as the theory's central predictive construct, and most recently reconceptualized the three-factor model and introduced the evolutionary framing of biologically primary/secondary knowledge. His willingness to revise his own theory (the 2010 germane load reconceptualization) exemplifies intellectual honesty in theoretical development.
> 
> **Key works:** Sweller (1988), Sweller et al. (1998), Sweller (2010), Sweller (2011)

> [!person] **Fred Paas (b. 1963) — Erasmus University Rotterdam** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Co-developer of the three-factor model and pioneer of cognitive load measurement methodology. [[fred-paas|Paas]] developed the widely used 9-point mental effort rating scale (1992) and co-authored the influential reformulation distinguishing intrinsic, extraneous, and germane load. His work on measurement has been foundational for empirical CLT research, even as the limitations of subjective measurement continue to challenge the field.
> 
> **Key works:** Paas (1992), Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas (1998), Paas et al. (2003)

> [!person] **Jeroen van Merriënboer (b. 1959) — Maastricht University** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Co-developer of the three-factor model and creator of the [[four-component-instructional-design-4cid|4C/ID (Four-Component Instructional Design)]] model. [[jeroen-van-merriënboer|Van Merriënboer]] translated CLT principles into a comprehensive instructional design methodology for complex learning environments, bridging the gap between theory and practice. He has also maintained the theoretical value of the germane load construct against Sweller's reconceptualization.
> 
> **Key works:** van Merriënboer (1997), Sweller, van Merriënboer & Paas (1998), van Merriënboer & Kirschner (2018)

> [!person] **Sergei Kalyuga — University of New South Wales** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Principal researcher on the [[expertise-reversal-effect|expertise reversal effect]] and adaptive instruction. [[Sergei-Kalyuga|Kalyuga's]] work demonstrated that virtually all CLT effects reverse with increasing expertise, establishing one of the theory's most important boundary conditions and motivating the research program on adaptive CLT-based instruction.
> 
> **Key works:** Kalyuga et al. (2003), Kalyuga (2007), Kalyuga (2011)

> [!person] **Richard Mayer (b. 1947) — UC Santa Barbara** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Developer of the [[cognitive-theory-of-multimedia-learning]], which independently derives many principles parallel to CLT effects from similar cognitive architecture assumptions. [[Richard-Mayer|Mayer's]] work on multimedia learning principles has provided the most extensive applied evidence base for CLT-adjacent design principles, and his research program has been instrumental in extending cognitive load considerations into technology-enhanced learning environments.
> 
> **Key works:** Mayer (2001, 2009), Mayer & Moreno (2003)

> [!person] **Paul Chandler — University of New South Wales (deceased)** *(from [[cognitive-load-theory-foundational-report-2026-04-12]])*
> Sweller's key early collaborator who co-developed the theoretical treatment of split attention and redundancy effects. Chandler's work was instrumental in establishing CLT's empirical effects catalog during the 1990s.
> 
> **Key works:** Chandler & Sweller (1991), Sweller & Chandler (1994)

> [!person] **Hermann Ebbinghaus (1850-1909)** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> German experimental psychologist who pioneered the quantitative study of memory. His self-experimental work (*Über das Gedächtnis*, 1885) established the forgetting curve, the spacing effect, and the method of savings in relearning. Ebbinghaus introduced the concept of the *nonsense syllable* as a controlled learning unit and invented the practice of learning-to-criterion paradigms. His empirical empiricism preceded the cognitive revolution by 70 years but established the quantitative, experimental character of memory research. Related figures: [[william-james]] (contemporary theorist), [[Georg-Elias-Müller]] (early collaborator in formal memory study).

> [!person] **Frederic Bartlett (1886-1969)** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> British psychologist whose *Remembering* (1932) demonstrated the constructive, schema-guided nature of memory through serial reproduction experiments. Bartlett challenged Ebbinghaus's decontextualized, atomistic approach and proposed that memory serves adaptive social and cognitive functions, not mere storage. His work was largely ignored for four decades, then recovered and became foundational to cognitive psychology in the schema-theoretic tradition. Related figures: [[endel-tulving]], [[Elizabeth-Loftus]].

> [!person] **Alan Baddeley (1934–)** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> British cognitive psychologist who, with Graham Hitch, proposed the multicomponent working memory model (1974) that replaced the Atkinson-Shiffrin short-term memory store. Baddeley's subsequent four decades of work elaborated each component of the model, added the episodic buffer (2000), and integrated the model with neuropsychological evidence. His contributions defined the research program of working memory for five decades. Related figure: [[graham-hitch]], [[Nelson-Cowan]] (alternative models).

> [!person] **Endel Tulving (1927–)** *(from [[memory-systems-working-memory-and-long-term-memory-foundational-report-2026-04-07]])*
> Estonian-Canadian cognitive psychologist at the University of Toronto who proposed the episodic-semantic memory distinction (1972), developed the encoding specificity principle with Donald Thomson (1973), introduced the concept of autonoetic consciousness as the phenomenological mark of episodic memory, and conducted foundational work on the neuroscience of episodic memory. Tulving's theoretical innovations — establishing the multiple memory systems framework for declarative memory — are arguably the most influential contributions to LTM theory in the twentieth century. Related figures: [[fergus-i.-m.-craik]], [[larry-squire]], [[Elizabeth-Loftus]].
